Q-ball solutions presented in Section
6.5
.

Comparing these results to the numerically calculated values of energy and charge in Tables
6.1
and
6.2
it is clear that the analytical estimates (
6.134
) and (
6.138
) consistently underestimate the energy and charge by about an order of magnitude for most of the Q-ball solutions. This makes sense when one considers the fact that the analytical expressions are only integrated to
r=r_{Q}/2
, as this is the limit at which the analytical approximation ceases to be a valid description of the behaviour of the Q-ball solution. The underestimate becomes greater as
\phi_{0}
decreases, which is consistent with the analytical Q-ball profile
\phi(r)
becoming a poorer fit to the numerical profile as
\phi_{0}
approaches the edge of plateau, and therefore the limit of plateau description of the inflaton field dynamics. However, since the analytical approximation is a good approximation for the behaviour of the Q-ball solutions with larger
\phi_{0}
, it can be used as an estimate of the properties of these Q-balls and to examine the dependence of these quantities on the other parameters of the model.

6.6.2
Analytical Derivation of the Q-ball Chemical Potential Relation

In this section we derive the energy per unit charge for Palatini non-minimally coupled Q-balls and demonstrate that this is can be equated to the
\omega
parameter in this model. This discussion follows very closely the derivation in Heeck et. al.
[
170
]
for the case of conventional minimally coupled Q-balls.

As a starting point we consider the Q-ball energy functional (
6.72
)

E_{Q}=\int 4\pi r^{2}dr\left[\frac{\omega^{2}\phi^{2}}{2\Omega^{2}}+\frac{1}{2\Omega^{2}}\left(\frac{\partial\phi}{\partial r}\right)^{2}+\frac{V}{\Omega^{4}}\right]+\omega Q-\omega\int 4\pi r^{2}dr\rho_{Q},

(6.140)

where

\rho_{Q}=\frac{\omega\phi^{2}}{\Omega^{2}}.

(6.141)

Absorbing the
\rho_{Q}
integral in (
6.140
) into the first integral and rewriting the
\omega
term, this becomes

E_{Q}=\int 4\pi r^{2}dr\left[\frac{1}{2\Omega^{2}}\left(\frac{\partial\phi}{\partial r}\right)^{2}+\frac{\omega^{2}\phi^{2}}{\Omega^{2}}-\frac{\omega^{2}\phi^{2}}{2\Omega^{2}}-\frac{\omega^{2}\phi^{2}}{\Omega^{2}}+\frac{V}{\Omega^{4}}\right]+\omega Q.

(6.142)

We now differentiate (
6.142
) with respect to
\omega
,

\frac{dE_{Q}}{d\omega}=\int 4\pi r^{2}dr\frac{\partial}{\partial\omega}\left[\frac{1}{2\Omega^{2}}\left(\frac{\partial\phi}{\partial r}\right)^{2}+\frac{\omega^{2}\phi^{2}}{\Omega^{2}}-\frac{\omega^{2}\phi^{2}}{2\Omega^{2}}-\frac{\omega^{2}\phi^{2}}{\Omega^{2}}+\frac{V}{\Omega^{4}}\right]+Q+\omega\frac{dQ}{d\omega},

(6.143)

and rewrite the integral using the field rescaling outlined in Section
6.4.1
to proceed with this part of the calculation in terms of
\sigma

\frac{dE_{Q}}{d\omega}=\int 4\pi r^{2}dr\left[\frac{1}{2}\frac{\partial}{\partial\omega}\left(\frac{\partial\sigma}{\partial r}\right)^{2}+\frac{\partial}{\partial\omega}\left(\frac{\omega^{2}\phi^{2}}{\Omega^{2}}\right)-\frac{\omega^{2}}{2}\frac{\partial}{\partial\omega}\left(\frac{\phi^{2}}{\Omega^{2}}\right)\right.\\
\left.-\frac{\partial}{\partial\omega}\left(\frac{\omega^{2}\phi^{2}}{\Omega^{2}}\right)+\frac{\partial}{\partial\omega}\left(\frac{V}{\Omega^{4}}\right)\right]+\omega\frac{dQ}{d\omega}.

(6.144)

Rescaling the Q-ball equation in terms of
\sigma
(
6.90
),we find that the left-hand side can be written

\frac{d^{2}\sigma}{dr^{2}}+\frac{2}{r}\frac{d\sigma}{dr}=\frac{1}{r^{2}}\frac{d}{dr}\left(r^{2}\frac{d\sigma}{dr}\right),

(6.145)

which can then be substituted back into the Q-ball equation (
6.90
) and rearranged to give

\frac{\partial}{\partial\sigma}\left(\frac{V}{\Omega^{4}}\right)=\frac{1}{r^{2}}\frac{d}{dr}\left(r^{2}\frac{d\sigma}{dr}\right)+\frac{\partial}{\partial\sigma}\left(\frac{\omega^{2}\phi^{2}}{2\Omega^{2}}\right).

(6.146)

We can write the left-hand side of (
6.146
)

\frac{\partial}{\partial\omega}\left(\frac{V}{\Omega^{4}}\right)=\frac{\partial}{\partial\sigma}\left(\frac{V}{\Omega^{4}}\right)\frac{\partial\sigma}{\partial\omega},

(6.147)

\Rightarrow\frac{\partial}{\partial\omega}\left(\frac{V}{\Omega^{4}}\right)=\frac{1}{r^{2}}\frac{d}{dr}\left(r^{2}\frac{d\sigma}{dr}\right)\frac{\partial\sigma}{\partial\omega}+\frac{\partial}{\partial\sigma}\left(\frac{\omega^{2}\phi^{2}}{2\Omega^{2}}\right)\frac{\partial\sigma}{\partial\omega}.

(6.148)

Substituting (
6.148
) back into (
6.144
) gives

\frac{dE_{Q}}{d\omega}=\int 4\pi r^{2}dr\left[\frac{1}{2}\frac{\partial}{\partial\omega}\left(\frac{\partial\sigma}{\partial r}\right)\left(\frac{\partial\sigma}{\partial r}\right)-\frac{\omega^{2}}{2}\frac{\partial}{\partial\omega}\left(\frac{\phi^{2}}{\Omega^{2}}\right)+\frac{1}{r^{2}}\frac{d}{dr}\left(r^{2}\frac{d\sigma}{dr}\right)\frac{\partial\sigma}{\partial\omega}\right.\\
\left.+\frac{\partial}{\partial\sigma}\left(\frac{\omega^{2}\phi^{2}}{2\Omega^{2}}\right)\frac{\partial\sigma}{\partial\omega}\right]+\omega\frac{dQ}{d\omega},

(6.149)

after canceling the
\pm\frac{\partial}{\partial\omega}\left(\frac{\omega^{2}\phi^{2}}{\Omega^{2}}\right)
terms. Rewriting the second term in (
6.149
)

\frac{\omega^{2}}{2}\frac{\partial}{\partial\omega}\left(\frac{\phi^{2}}{\Omega^{2}}\right)=\frac{\omega^{2}}{2}\frac{\partial}{\partial\sigma}\left(\frac{\phi^{2}}{\Omega^{2}}\right)\frac{\partial\sigma}{\partial\omega},

(6.150)

and canceling with the fourth term in (
6.149
) leaves

\frac{dE_{Q}}{d\omega}=\int 4\pi r^{2}dr\left[\frac{1}{2}\frac{\partial}{\partial\omega}\left(\frac{\partial\sigma}{\partial r}\right)\left(\frac{\partial\sigma}{\partial r}\right)+\frac{1}{r^{2}}\frac{d}{dr}\left(r^{2}\frac{d\sigma}{dr}\right)\frac{\partial\sigma}{\partial\omega}\right]+\omega\frac{dQ}{d\omega}.

(6.151)

We then integrate the second term by parts

\int\frac{1}{r^{2}}\frac{d}{dr}\left(r^{2}\frac{d\sigma}{dr}\right)\frac{\partial\sigma}{\partial\omega}dr=\left.\frac{\partial\sigma}{\partial r}\frac{\partial\sigma}{\partial\omega}\right|-\int\frac{\partial\sigma}{\partial r}\frac{\partial^{2}\sigma}{\partial r\partial\omega},

(6.152)

and obtain

\frac{dE_{Q}}{d\omega}=\int 4\pi r^{2}dr\left[\frac{1}{2}\frac{\partial}{\partial\omega}\left(\frac{\partial\sigma}{\partial r}\right)\left(\frac{\partial\sigma}{\partial r}\right)-\frac{\partial\sigma}{\partial r}\frac{\partial^{2}\sigma}{\partial r\partial\omega}\right]+\omega\frac{dQ}{d\omega},

(6.153)

where the boundary term from the integration is neglected due to the fact that the gradient of the scalar field goes to zero at infinity. The remaining terms in the integrand cancel and we are left with precisely

\frac{dE_{Q}}{d\omega}=\omega\frac{dQ}{d\omega}.

(6.154)

This is the same result as derived in
[
170
]
for the case of a conventional canonical scalar, and shows that for Q-balls in non-minimally coupled Palatini gravity, the
\omega
parameter can be equated to the chemical potential of the system

\frac{dE_{Q}}{dQ}=\omega,

(6.155)

and that
E_{Q}\left(\omega\right)
and
Q\left(\omega\right)
increase and decrease together, as in the case of conventional Q-balls
[
50
]
.

This makes sense in terms of
\omega
being the Lagrange multiplier, since for a given
Q
there will be a value of
\omega
which extremises the energy of the scalar field subject to the constraint of constant charge. Hence
Q
is a function of
\omega
, and extremising the scalar field for a fixed
Q(\omega)
will produce an energy configuration
E_{Q}(\omega)
corresponding to a Q-ball.

It is also straightforward to show analytically that the
\omega
parameter is approximately the ratio of the energy to the global charge. The following derivation closely follows that of the derivation of Eq.
12
in Heeck et al.
[
170
]
for the case of conventional Q-balls.

From (
6.72
), the effective Q-ball action can be written

\int dr\;\mathcal{L_{Q}}=\int 4\pi r^{2}\;dr\left[\frac{1}{2}\left(\frac{d\sigma}{dr}\right)^{2}-\frac{\omega^{2}\phi^{2}}{2\Omega^{2}}+\frac{V}{\Omega^{4}}\right],

(6.156)

where the inflaton is understood as being a function of the rescaled field
\sigma
in this case. We then perform a coordinate rescaling
r\rightarrow\chi\tilde{r}
, and write (
6.156
) in terms of
\tilde{r}

\int d\tilde{r}\;\mathcal{L_{Q}}=\int 4\pi\;d\tilde{r}\;\tilde{r}^{2}\;\chi^{3}\,\left[\frac{1}{2\chi^{2}}\left(\frac{\partial\sigma}{\partial r}\right)^{2}-\frac{\omega^{2}\phi^{2}}{2\Omega^{2}}+\frac{V}{\Omega^{4}}\right].

(6.157)

This (
6.157
) contains an explicit dependence on
\chi
from the factors present in the integrand, and an implicit dependence on
\chi
, since the quasi-canonical field is a function of
r
and transforms as
\sigma\left(r\right)\rightarrow\sigma\left(\chi\tilde{r}\right)
, meaning that a small variation in
\chi
will cause a small variation in
\sigma
. This means that we can write the variation of the Q-ball effective Lagrangian with respect to
\chi
as

\frac{d\mathcal{L_{Q}}}{d\chi}=\left(\frac{d\mathcal{L_{Q}}}{d\chi}\right)_{explicit}+\left(\frac{d\mathcal{L_{Q}}}{d\chi}\right)_{implicit},

(6.158)

where
\chi=1
corresponds to a Q-ball solution which extremises the Q-ball effective action. This means that
\int d\tilde{r}\left(\frac{d\mathcal{L}_{Q}}{d\chi}\right)_{implicit}=0
at
\chi=1
. Since the value of (
6.157
) is unchanged following the rescaling of the radial coordinate, we require that

\int d\tilde{r}\;\left(\frac{d\mathcal{L}_{Q}}{d\chi}\right)_{explicit}=0,

(6.159)

when
\chi=1
. The left-hand side of this expression is

\int d\tilde{r}\;\left(\frac{d\mathcal{L}_{Q}}{d\chi}\right)_{explicit}=4\pi\int d\tilde{r}\;\frac{\partial}{\partial\chi}\left[\frac{\chi}{2}\left(\frac{d\sigma}{dr}\right)^{2}-\frac{\chi^{3}\omega^{2}\phi^{2}}{2\Omega^{2}}+\frac{\chi^{3}V}{\Omega^{4}}\right]\\
=4\pi\int\;d\tilde{r}\;\tilde{r}^{2}\left[\frac{1}{2}\left(\frac{d\sigma}{d\tilde{r}}\right)^{2}+3\chi^{2}\left(\frac{V}{\Omega^{4}}-\frac{\omega^{2}\phi^{2}}{2\Omega^{2}}\right)\right],

(6.160)

which means that for
\chi=1,\tilde{r}\rightarrow r
, and in order for (
6.159
) to be satisfied we require that

4\pi\int dr\;r^{2}\left[\frac{1}{2}\left(\frac{d\sigma}{dr}\right)^{2}+3\left(\frac{V}{\Omega^{4}}-\frac{\omega^{2}\phi^{2}}{2\Omega^{2}}\right)\right]=0.

(6.161)

This can be rewritten to obtain the condition

\begin{split}4\pi\int dr\;r^{2}\left[\frac{1}{2}\left(\frac{d\sigma}{dr}\right)^{2}+\frac{3V}{\Omega^{4}}\right]=4\pi\int r^{2}&\;dr\frac{3\omega^{2}\phi^{2}}{2\Omega^{2}}\\
&=4\pi\cdot\frac{3}{2}\int r^{2}\;dr\frac{\omega^{2}\phi^{2}}{\Omega^{2}}=\frac{3}{2}\omega Q,\end{split}

(6.162)

\Rightarrow 4\pi\int dr\;r^{2}\left[\frac{1}{2}\left(\frac{d\sigma}{dr}\right)^{2}+3\left(\frac{V}{\Omega^{4}}\right)\right]=\frac{3}{2}\omega Q.

(6.163)

The Q-ball energy functional (
6.72
) can be written

E_{Q}=\omega Q+4\pi\int dr\;r^{2}\left[\frac{1}{2}\left(\frac{d\sigma}{dr}\right)^{2}+\frac{V}{\Omega^{4}}\right]-4\pi\int dr\;r^{2}\frac{\omega^{2}\phi^{2}}{2\Omega^{2}},

(6.164)

where the last term is precisely

4\pi\int dr\;r^{2}\frac{\omega^{2}\phi^{2}}{2\Omega^{2}}=\frac{1}{2}\omega Q.

(6.165)

Using (
6.163
) we can write

\frac{1}{2}\omega Q=4\pi\int dr\;r^{2}\left[\frac{1}{6}\left(\frac{d\sigma}{dr}\right)^{2}+\frac{V}{\Omega^{4}}\right],

(6.166)

which, substituted into the Q-ball energy functional (
6.164
), gives

E_{Q}=\omega Q+4\pi\int dr\;r^{2}\left[\frac{1}{2}\left(\frac{d\sigma}{dr}\right)^{2}+\frac{V}{\Omega^{4}}\right]-4\pi\int dr\;r^{2}\left[\frac{1}{6}\left(\frac{d\sigma}{dr}\right)^{2}+\frac{V}{\Omega^{4}}\right],

(6.167)

which is the expression for the energy of the field extremised with respect to the global charge
Q

E_{Q}=\omega Q+\frac{4\pi}{3}\int dr\;r^{2}\left(\frac{d\sigma}{dr}\right)^{2}.

(6.168)

Thus, the energy of the Q-ball is, in terms of
\phi
,

E_{Q}=\omega Q+\frac{4\pi}{3}\int dr\;r^{2}\frac{1}{\Omega^{2}}\left(\frac{d\phi}{dr}\right)^{2},

(6.169)

where the integral can be interpreted as a surface energy term. There are some interesting things which can be said about this relation. Dividing through by the charge gives

\frac{E_{Q}}{Q}=\omega+\frac{4\pi}{3Q}\int dr\;r^{2}\frac{1}{\Omega^{2}}\left(\frac{d\phi}{dr}\right)^{2},

(6.170)

which shows that the
\omega
parameter is equal to the energy-charge ratio of the Q-ball up to a small correction due to a contribution from the surface energy of the Q-ball. In the limit
\Omega\rightarrow 1
, this expression is equal to that obtained for conventional Q-balls in
[
170
]
. It also provides some analytical verification of the condition for absolute stability. If the Q-ball is absolutely stable,
E_{Q}<mQ
, then

\frac{E_{Q}}{Q}=\omega+\frac{4\pi}{3Q}\int dr\;r^{2}\frac{1}{\Omega^{2}}\left(\frac{d\phi}{dr}\right)^{2}<m.

(6.171)

Provided that the surface term is significantly smaller than
\omega
, we find that

\omega<m,

(6.172)

is an alternative formulation of the absolute stability condition for these Q-balls. Since
\omega<m
in order for Q-ball solutions to exist, it follows that if (
6.172
) holds then all Q-ball solutions will be stable.

6.6.3
Application of the Energy-Charge Relation to the Analytical Approximate Q-ball Solution

In this section we use the analytical Q-ball solution to show that the energy to charge ratio is close to
\omega
for
\phi_{0}>>M_{pl}/\sqrt{\xi}
, confirming the numerical results in Figure
6.12
. The analytical approximation for the scalar field is given by (
6.125
) and the analytical expression for the Q-ball charge is given by (
6.138
). The analytical approximation of
\phi
is valid provided that the field does not deviate too far from its initial value,
\phi\approx\phi_{0}
, which is true for
r<r_{Q}/2
. The analytical expression for the charge is calculated for
r<r_{Q}/2
, so the integral in this expression must be taken as far as
r=r_{Q}/2
.

Differentiating the inflaton field in the analytical approximation (
6.125
) gives

\frac{d\phi}{dr}=-\frac{2r\phi_{0}}{r_{Q}^{2}}\Rightarrow\left(\frac{d\phi}{dr}\right)^{2}=\frac{4r^{2}\phi_{0}^{2}}{r_{Q}^{4}}.

(6.173)

Substituting this into (
6.171
) in the plateau limit
\Omega^{2}\approx\xi\phi_{0}^{2}/M_{pl}^{2}
, and the charge
Q
from (
6.138
) gives

\frac{E}{Q}=\omega+\frac{4\pi\cdot 6\xi}{\pi\omega M_{pl}^{2}}\frac{1}{r_{Q}^{3}}\int^{r_{Q}/2}_{0}r^{2}\;dr\frac{M_{pl}^{2}}{3\xi\phi_{0}^{2}}\frac{4r^{2}\phi_{0}^{2}}{r_{Q}^{4}}=\omega+\frac{32}{\omega r_{Q}^{7}}\int^{r_{Q}/2}_{0}r^{4}dr.

(6.174)

Performing the integration in (
6.174
) we obtain

\frac{E}{Q}=\omega+\frac{1}{5\omega}\frac{1}{r_{Q}^{2}}.

(6.175)

Substituting in the expression for
r_{Q}^{2}
from (
6.132
) gives

\frac{E}{Q}=\omega+\frac{1}{30\omega}\frac{M_{pl}^{2}}{\xi\phi_{0}^{2}}\left(m^{2}+\omega^{2}-\omega_{c}^{2}\right).

(6.176)

For an inflaton mass within the Q-ball window (
6.108
),
m^{2}+\omega^{2}-\omega_{c}^{2}\leq m^{2}
, and we have

\frac{E}{Q}\leq\omega+\frac{\omega}{30}\frac{M_{pl}^{2}}{\xi\phi_{0}^{2}}\left[1+\frac{m^{2}}{\omega^{2}}\right].

(6.177)

Taking
m^{2}\approx\omega^{2}
, we have that, at most

\frac{E}{Q}=\omega\left[1+\mathcal{O}\left(\frac{M_{pl}^{2}}{\xi\phi_{0}^{2}}\right)\right],

(6.178)

and therefore

\frac{E}{Q}\thickapprox\omega,

(6.179)

for
\phi_{0}>>M_{pl}/\sqrt{\xi}
. This means that we can safely say to a very good approximation that the
\omega
parameter in this model is the energy-charge ratio for
\phi_{0}>>M_{pl}/\sqrt{\xi}
.

6.6.4
Analytical Dependence of Q-ball Energy and Radius on the Inflaton Self-Coupling

The
\gamma
parameter as defined (
6.115
) is

\gamma=\frac{M_{pl}^{2}}{\xi}\left(m^{2}+\omega^{2}-\omega_{c}^{2}\right),

(6.180)

and
\omega_{c}^{2}=\lambda M_{pl}^{2}/\xi
. If we approximate
m^{2}\approx\omega^{2}\approx\omega_{c}^{2}
, we have that

\gamma\approx\frac{M_{pl}^{2}}{\xi}\omega_{c}^{2}=\frac{\lambda M_{pl}^{4}}{\xi^{2}}\approx 4V_{E},

(6.181)

from (
6.33
) in the plateau approximation.

The primordial curvature power spectrum is given by

\mathcal{P}_{\mathcal{R}}=\frac{\lambda N^{2}}{12\pi^{2}\xi},

(6.182)

and this can be rearranged to give an expression for the inflaton self-coupling,
\lambda
,

\lambda=\left(\frac{12\pi^{2}P_{R}}{N^{2}}\right)\xi\Rightarrow\lambda\propto\xi.

(6.183)

Using this, from (
6.181
), we have that

\gamma\propto\frac{\lambda}{\lambda^{2}}\Rightarrow\gamma\sim\frac{1}{\lambda}.

(6.184)

This means that, taking the expression for
r_{Q}
from (
6.124
), we can establish the dependence of
r_{Q}
on the inflaton self-coupling

r_{Q}=\frac{\sqrt{6}\phi_{0}}{\sqrt{\gamma}}\Rightarrow r_{Q}\propto\sqrt{\lambda}\phi_{0}.

(6.185)

The analytical expression for the Q-ball energy is given by (
6.134
). Since in general
\omega^{2}\approx\omega_{c}^{2}
, which follows from
m^{2}\simeq\omega^{2}
and (
6.108
), the energy of the Q-ball becomes

E\approx\frac{3}{2}\frac{\pi M_{pl}^{2}}{12\xi}\omega_{c}^{2}r_{Q}^{3}\propto\frac{\omega_{c}^{2}r_{Q}^{3}}{\xi}\sim\frac{\lambda}{\xi}\frac{r_{Q}^{3}}{\xi},

(6.186)

\Rightarrow E\propto\sqrt{\lambda}\phi_{0}^{3}.

(6.187)

These relations will become important in Section
6.8
when we consider the effects of curvature on non-minimally coupled Palatini Q-balls.

6.7
Formation of Non-Minimally Coupled Palatini Q-balls Following Tachyonic Preheating

As discussed in Section
6.1
, we will consider the possibility, indicated by the results in
[
161
]
, that the Q-balls in this model may arise as a result of the fragmentation of the inflationary condensate, where the inflationary condensate breaks into smaller compact lumps of scalars which subsequently decay into
\pm
Q-ball pairs. If the inflationary potential is “flatter than
\phi^{2}
”, then this in general results in an attractive interaction between the scalars which creates a negative pressure within the inflaton condensate. This is one of the conditions which serves as a prerequisite for condensate fragmentation.

A requirement is that there is a tachyonic instability in the inflationary potential (second derivative of the effective potential/effective mass squared term is less than zero). This is satisfied on the plateau of the inflaton potential. When the field passes the tachyonic instability, quantum fluctuations of the field will be amplified as the field rolls quickly down its effective potential from the top of the local maximum. This tachyonic growth of certain modes during inflation can cause the condensate to fragment, provided that the perturbations in the energy density of the condensate are not diluted away by expansion before the tachyonic growth becomes significant. However, if there are no non-topological soliton solutions present in the scalar field theory of the inflaton then the condensate will not fragment and any large perturbations induced from tachyonic preheating will be smoothed out by expansion.

It has been shown in
[
169
]
for Palatini inflation with a real scalar field that the inflaton fluctuations grow rapidly due to tachyonic preheating at the end of slow-roll inflation, where this tachyonic growth occurs within a single inflaton oscillation for non-minimal couplings of the size relevant to this study. The non-linear fluctuations generated then become the dominant contribution to the dynamics of the inflaton field after growing very rapidly, and it is these amplified non-linear fluctuations which may cause the inflaton condensate to become unstable and fragment. In
[
169
]
, the analysis is undertaken for a
\phi^{4}
inflaton potential, which means that any amplified perturbations will be smoothed out somewhat more than in our case due to the repulsive interaction between the scalars dominating the dynamics of the inflaton within the condensate.

This analysis from
[
169
]
can therefore be applied to our model to provide a lower estimate on the range of perturbation wavelengths which undergo tachyonic growth and become non-linear. This provides a useful check on whether tachyonic preheating could be the underlying mechanism behind the formation of Q-balls in non-minimally coupled Palatini inflation, since if this is the case then the range of wavelengths of the amplified non-linear perturbations in this model should overlap with the radii of the relevant Q-balls generated in our numerical analysis (see Table
6.2
). Namely, we consider the radius of the
\phi_{0}=3.2217991\times 10^{17}{\rm\ GeV}
Q-ball, since this is within the range of estimated field values at the end of Palatini inflation, in order to estimate whether these Q-balls are likely to form from the fragmentation of the inflaton condensate following tachyonic preheating.

The inflaton begins to undergo oscillations when slow-roll inflation ends. We find that the value of the inflaton field at the onset of these oscillations in the model outlined in Section
6.3
is

\phi_{end}=2\sqrt{2}M_{pl}\sqrt{\beta},

(6.188)

where
\beta=0.1-1
, which gives
\phi_{end}\sim(1-3)M_{pl}
for the inflaton masses relevant to this work. When the non-linear fragments form we can expect that the value of the field will actually be less than this due to expansion taking place while tachyonic preheating is occurring. Field values in the range
10^{17}-10^{18}{\rm\ GeV}
are therefore a reasonable estimate.
For the purpose of this study we utilise a quantity introduced in
[
169
]
defined as the perturbation wavenumber

\kappa=\sqrt{\frac{\xi}{\lambda}}\frac{k}{M_{pl}},

(6.189)

which is a rescaling of the physical wavenumber
k
. The range of perturbation wavelengths for which strong tachyonic growth of the perturbations occurs is given by
\kappa_{min}<\kappa<\kappa_{max}
, where the authors in
[
169
]
define
\kappa_{max}\thickapprox 0.4a

[
169
]
, where
a
is the scale factor.
\kappa_{min}
corresponds to the size of the horizon. This range in perturbation wavenumber corresponds to a range of physical wavelengths,
\lambda_{min}<\lambda<\lambda_{max}
, for which the perturbations are expected to become non-linear.
\lambda_{min}
is determined by
\kappa_{max}
and
\lambda_{max}=2\pi H^{-1}
.

The maximum physical wavenumber
k/a
from (
6.189
) is

\frac{k_{max}}{a}=\sqrt{\frac{\lambda}{\xi}}\frac{\kappa_{max}}{a}M_{Pl}=\frac{2\pi}{\lambda_{min}},

(6.190)

and using
\kappa_{max}=0.4a
, we find that the minimum physical wavelength of the perturbations which will become non-linear due to tachyonic preheating is

\lambda_{min}=\frac{2\pi}{0.4}\sqrt{\frac{\xi}{\lambda}}M_{pl}^{-1}\equiv 15.7\sqrt{\frac{\xi}{\lambda}}M_{pl}^{-1}.

(6.191)

Normalising the non-minimal coupling and the inflaton self-coupling using
\xi=1.2\times 10^{9}
and
\lambda=0.1
respectively, we can write this as

\lambda_{min}=6.5\times 10^{-13}\left(\frac{0.1}{\lambda}\right)^{1/2}\left(\frac{\xi}{10^{9}}\right)^{\frac{1}{2}}{\rm\ GeV}^{-1}.

(6.192)

Taking
\lambda=0.1
and
\xi=1.2\times 10^{9}
we find that the smallest physical wavelength in this model which we expect to produce non-linear perturbations in the inflaton condensate is
\lambda_{min}=7.16\times 10^{-13}{\rm\ GeV}^{-1}
.

The Hubble parameter at the end of slow roll inflation is

H^{2}=\frac{V_{E}}{3M_{pl}^{2}},

(6.193)

where we take
V_{E}
to be the plateau approximation of the potential in the Einstein frame

V_{E}=\frac{\lambda M_{pl}^{4}}{4\xi^{2}}.

(6.194)

Substituting (
6.194
) into (
6.193
) we find that

H^{2}=\frac{\lambda}{12\xi^{2}}M_{pl}^{2}.

(6.195)

This means that

H^{-1}=\left(\frac{12}{\lambda}\right)^{\frac{1}{2}}\frac{\xi}{M_{pl}}=4.56\times 10^{-9}\left(\frac{0.1}{\lambda}\right)^{1/2}\left(\frac{\xi}{10^{9}}\right){\rm\ GeV}^{-1},

(6.196)

using the normalisation of (
6.192
). Taking
\lambda=0.1
and
\xi=1.2\times 10^{9}
, we find
H^{-1}=5.47\times 10^{-9}{\rm\ GeV}^{-1}
, and the maximum physical perturbation wavelength is then

\lambda_{max}=2\pi H^{-1}=2.87\times 10^{-8}\left(\frac{0.1}{\lambda}\right)^{1/2}\left(\frac{\xi}{10^{9}}\right){\rm\ GeV}^{-1}.

(6.197)

For
\lambda=0.1
and
\xi=1.2\times 10^{9}
, this gives the maximum physical wavelength of the perturbations which will become non-linear in this model to be
\lambda_{max}=3.4\times 10^{-8}{\rm\ GeV}^{-1}
.

We find therefore that strong perturbation growth occurs for modes with wavelengths in the range
7.16\times 10^{-13}{\rm\ GeV}^{-1}<\lambda<3.4\times 10^{-8}{\rm\ GeV}^{-1}
, using
\lambda=0.1
and
\xi=1.2\times 10^{9}
as our parameter estimates for the non-minimally coupled Palatini inflation model. The radii of all of the Q-ball solutions presented in Section
6.5
fall within this range. Most notably, the
\phi_{0}=3.2217991\times 10^{17}{\rm\ GeV}
Q-ball has a radius
r_{X}=8.89\times 10^{-10}{\rm\ GeV}^{-1}
, which is comfortably within the range of mode wavelengths we expect to see undergoing tachyonic growth. It is therefore possible that
\phi_{0}\sim 10^{17}-10^{18}{\rm\ GeV}
Q-balls could form in this model as a result of the fragmentation of the inflaton condensate due to tachyonic preheating, assuming that all other conditions for fragmentation are met, which means that non-minimally coupled Palatini Q-balls are physically feasible for the model parameters considered here, although a numerical simulation of the dynamics and fragmentation of the inflaton condensate in an expanding background would be needed in order to establish tachyonic preheating as a formation mechanism for these non-minimally coupled Palatini Q-balls properly.

6.8
The Effects of Curvature on Non-Minimally Coupled Palatini Q-balls

In this analysis we have studied Q-balls in flat space, as they have been derived historically. In this section we consider the possible effects of curvature on these Q-ball solutions, in order to ascertain the validity of the flat space solutions. As a first pass approximation, we conduct this analysis as though the Q-balls form in flat space and then curvature is switched on. In this approximation we can consider the Q-ball solution to have a spherically symmetric energy density. This means that the spacetime outside of the Q-ball is described by the Schwarzschild metric

ds^{2}=\left(1-\frac{2GM(r)}{r}\right)dt^{2}-\left(1-\frac{2GM(r)}{r}\right)^{-1}dr^{2}-r^{2}d\Omega^{2},

(6.198)

where
M(r)
is the mass-energy contained within radius
r
. Let the radius of the Q-ball in this scenario be
R
. If the radius of the Q-ball is smaller than its Schwarzschild radius,
r_{S}=2GM\left(r\right)
, then the Q-ball will collapse to form a black hole. The condition to ensure that the Q-balls are not signifcantly affected by the curvature of the surrounding spacetime is therefore

R>2GM(R)\equiv r_{S},

(6.199)

assuming that the mass-energy
M\left(r\right)
increases faster with
r
than
r
increases. Essentially this means that the flat space Q-ball solutions are a good approximation of non-minimally coupled Palatini Q-balls provided that the Q-balls are sufficiently large compared to their Schwarzschild radii
r_{S}
.

Setting
R=r_{Z}
and
M\left(r\right)=E
from Table
6.1
, we find that this condition is easily satisfied for all of the Q-balls which have been generated numerically in this model (see Table
6.4
). This means that if curvature is switched on, all of the Q-ball solutions presented in Table
6.1
remain as Q-balls and do not collapse to black holes. As a first approximation, this means that the flat space description is reasonable. It has been shown for conventional canonical scalar field theories that spatial curvature can affect the size of Q-balls
[
167
]
, and the expression for the energy of the Q-balls will be different in curved space, since the energy density is derived from the energy-momentum tensor. In order to determine the full effects of gravity on these Q-balls, the Q-ball equation and the energy and charge of the Q-balls would need to be derived in a spherically symmetric spacetime. However, for the purposes of this thesis the flat space Q-ball solutions can be used to approximate the effects of curvature on Q-balls in a non-minimally coupled Palatini inflation framework.
